# Test ledger: every hypothesis test in the project, and what survives correction

This page is generated by `scripts/build_test_ledger.py` from the result files. Rerun the script after any study changes; the numbers below then update. The full ledger is `results/test_ledger.csv` (4930 rows, one per test). Results that are reported but deliberately not counted are in `results/test_ledger_not_counted.csv`, and the recomputation of every adjusted p-value that a study reports itself is in `results/test_ledger_checks.csv`. All results are REAL DATA unless a study says otherwise.

## 1. Why this ledger exists

A p-value answers one question: if there were no effect, how often would a result at least this extreme turn up by chance? At the usual 5% line, one test in twenty with no real effect behind it still comes out "significant". This project ran **4930 tests** across 17 studies. Even if none of the effects were real, about 246 of them would be expected to pass 5% by luck alone. In fact 1704 have a raw p below 0.05. The question for a reader is which of those would still stand if we account for how many shots we took.

**Multiple-testing corrections** raise each p-value to reflect the number of tests. Two are used here:

* **Benjamini-Yekutieli (BY)** controls the false discovery rate: among the results we call significant, the expected share of false ones stays below 5%. Unlike the better-known Benjamini-Hochberg method, BY is valid whatever the dependence between tests. That matters here, because many tests reuse the same days, the same GEX series and the same outcomes, so they are strongly correlated. The price is that BY is conservative: it multiplies by an extra factor c(m) = 1 + 1/2 + ... + 1/m, which for m = 4930 is 9.08. With 4930 tests, the smallest p-value must be below about 1.1e-06 to survive, and the k-th smallest below about k x 1.1e-06.
* **Holm** (within each study) controls the chance of even one false positive within that study. It is the standard, strict choice for a study's own family of tests.

We report three adjusted values for every test: BY over the whole project (`p_by_project`), BY within its study (`p_by_study`) and Holm within its study (`p_holm_study`). Project-wide BY is the strictest reading: it treats the project as one search. Within-study values are the fairer reading for a study that was designed on its own. Neither replaces judgement: a correlated set of tests that all point the same way can be more convincing than any single p-value suggests, and BY ignores that.

**How the ledger counts.** One row is one hypothesis: one estimate, in one sample, under one specification. Where a study reports several p-values for the same estimate (for example a HAC p, a bootstrap p and a placebo p), the one the study treats as main is used and the rest go in `alt_p`. Coefficients on control variables, pre-trend diagnostics and placebo draws are not tests. A test reported in two files is counted once. Raw p-values are two-sided unless the study stated a one-sided test in advance (column `sidedness`); a one-sided p without such a statement is doubled. No p-value is invented: a result shown only as a figure, an interval or text is listed as not counted.

**Pre-specified?** `yes` means a written statement of the test was committed to git before the run, judged from the commit times in `.git/logs/HEAD` (no git command is run; confirm with git log). That holds for RESEARCH_STRATEGY_PANDEY.md (RQ1 and H1), which was in the repository before the first real-data run, and for the P87 and P90 pre-registrations in `docs/prereg/` when their commit is older than the study's result files. Studies with `yes` rows now: F2ECON, PINMECH, R1. Every other study was committed together with its results, so a claim that a test was fixed in advance is marked `stated, not time-stamped`. `partly` means the direction was stated in advance but the exact specification was chosen later.

## 3. What survives project-wide BY at 5%

**747 of 4930 tests survive (15%). That is more than "very few", but it says little about the project's gamma claims, for three reasons.**

1. **The bar is set by the strong, unsurprising results.** BY steps up through the sorted p-values, so the cut-off depends on how many very small p-values there are. Here it works out at a raw p of about 8.0e-04: every test at or below that survives and none above it does. The project contains hundreds of near-certain effects: option and futures turnover jumping on expiry days (375 of the 550 event-study survivors are turnover measures), forecasting models beating mcsGARCH at 15 and 30 minutes, Sensex calendar checks, volume changes in the replication. Each one makes room for the next. BY's 5% promise is about the whole list of survivors, not about any subset such as the GEX results, where false positives could be more concentrated.
2. **The event study's HAC p-values reject too often.** Its own negative-control check (Fridays, no rule change) found HAC p below 0.05 in 20% to 46% of tests (EVENT_STUDY.md 6.2), and 24 of those Friday placebo rows survive here. The study itself trusts only results with a small placebo-date p, whose smallest possible value is about 0.005; no p-value that large can survive project-wide BY.
3. **Some survivors cut against a claim.** 84 survivors involve GEX, dealer gamma or the dealer sign (11 of them placebo rows). Several are falsification results: gamma measured after the outcome predicting as well as gamma measured before it, or gross gamma with no sign predicting volatility. A surviving placebo is evidence against the original claim.

Without the event study (2791 tests), 151 of the remaining 2139 tests would survive BY, with a cut-off of about 4.2e-04 on the raw p.

By study: EVENT 550, REPL 68, FALS 50, SENSEX 17, R1 16, INTRA 15, DSIGN 8, R5 7, AUCTION 6, PIN 4, PINMECH 3, R2 1, R3 1, CONN 1. By role: exploratory 377, post hoc 138, robustness 126, secondary 54, placebo 35, headline 17.

## 5. Reconciliation with the eight findings of REAL_DATA_RESULTS.md

For each finding: the ledger rows that carry it, and whether it survives BY across the whole project. REAL_DATA_RESULTS.md itself is not changed here.

**Finding 1.** The data hold up.

No p-values: this is data validation (results/data_validation), not a hypothesis test. Nothing to correct.

**Finding 2.** A hybrid of mcsGARCH and LightGBM beats mcsGARCH at 15 and 30 minutes, on all days and on expiry days (DM p of 0.001 or below).

| test_id | role | hypothesis | rule | sample | raw p | Holm study | BY project |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| R1-0035 | secondary | hybrid_base forecasts better than mcsGARCH (the paper's model) |  | all test days (n = 792), horizon next15 | 1.5e-12 | 1.2e-10 | 2.5e-10 |
| R1-0063 | secondary | hybrid_base forecasts better than mcsGARCH (the paper's model) |  | all test days (n = 792), horizon next30 | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| R1-0049 | headline | hybrid_base forecasts better than mcsGARCH (the paper's model) |  | expiry test days (n = 169), horizon next15 | 0.000668 | 0.046 | 0.041 |
| R1-0077 | headline | hybrid_base forecasts better than mcsGARCH (the paper's model) |  | expiry test days (n = 169), horizon next30 | 0.00024 | 0.017 | 0.017 |

* hybrid against mcsGARCH, all days: 2 of 2 rows survive (smallest raw p 0, smallest BY project 0, smallest Holm within study 0).
* hybrid against mcsGARCH, expiry days: 2 of 2 rows survive (smallest raw p 0.00024, smallest BY project 0.017, smallest Holm within study 0.017).

**Survives project-wide BY.**

**Finding 3.** Last hour: HAR is about 11% better over all days (one-sided p = 0.015), but not before the closing auction (p = 0.11).

| test_id | role | hypothesis | rule | sample | raw p | Holm study | BY project |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| R1-0004 | secondary | har forecasts better than mcsGARCH (the paper's model) |  | all test days (n = 792), horizon last_hour | 0.015 | 0.944 | 0.533 |
| AUCTION-0013 | robustness | har forecasts better than mcsGARCH, before the closing auction |  | all test days before 3 Aug 2026 (n = 729) | 0.108 | 1.000 | 1.000 |

* HAR against mcsGARCH, last hour, all days: 0 of 1 rows survive (smallest raw p 0.015, smallest BY project 0.533, smallest Holm within study 0.944).
* the same before 3 Aug 2026: 0 of 1 rows survive (smallest raw p 0.108, smallest BY project 1.000, smallest Holm within study 1.000).

**Does not survive project-wide BY.** The finding itself says the last-hour edge is not reliable.

## 6. Cross-checks against the studies' own adjusted p-values

Every adjusted p-value that a study reports was recomputed from that study's own raw p-values (39 families or variants, `results/test_ledger_checks.csv`). Results: match 38, MISMATCH 1.

| study | family | method | tests | mismatches | largest difference | note |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| FALS | 18 headline tests, recomputed from the current R3 and R5 files | holm | 18 | 3 | 0.000159 | the study's F2 rows used 932 days (older code); R5 now uses 929 |

**Reading the cross-checks.** A match means the study's adjusted p-values follow from its own raw p-values exactly as its documentation says. Known differences, all explained:

* **FALSIFICATION family A and the 18 headline tests** were computed from an older run of R3 and R5. The study's F2 rows use 932 days (931 with controls); the current R5 file uses 929. The p-values move a little (for example Pro binary 0.000214 then, 0.000216 now), so the study's "Holm 0.016 among 78" and "Holm 0.020 among 18" are reproduced from its own file but differ slightly when recomputed from the current R3 and R5 files. The row-level values are in the `note` column of the R3 and R5 headline rows.
* **Family A lists six tests twice** (the regime x expiry rows and the per-SD robustness rows of R3 have identical p-values; only the scale of the coefficient differs), so its "78 tests" are 72 distinct tests; its Holm values are therefore slightly conservative. The ledger counts each once.
* **Event study BH** is applied by the study to `hac_p` within each window, across all 34 comparisons including the Friday negative controls; the recomputation uses the same set.
* **Intraday gamma, Holm within family** counts rows that repeat each other (tests 120 and 121 repeat 118; 123 repeats 122), so its within-family Holm values are slightly conservative.
* **Falsification T4 forecast** p-values are one-sided; the study's Holm and BH use them as one-sided, while the ledger doubles them because the direction was not stated in advance.

### 7.3 Warnings from the last run

* pinning_mechanism/settlement_check.csv: not read by the PINMECH adapter; check whether it holds tests

## 8. Column guide for `results/test_ledger.csv`

`study`, `test_id`, `hypothesis` (short plain English), `outcome`, `sample`, `sign_rule`, `estimate`, `statistic_name`, `statistic`, `p_reported` (as in the source file), `p_raw` (two-sided unless `sidedness` says one-sided and pre-stated), `sidedness`, `p_method`, `alt_p` (other p-values of the same estimate, not counted), `role`, `pre_specified`, `pre_spec_note`, `source_file`, `source_key` (the row's identifying fields in that file), `p_study_adjusted` and `study_adjust_method` (the study's own adjusted p, where it reports one), `p_study_adjusted_recomputed` and `study_adjust_check`, `p_by_project`, `p_by_study`, `p_holm_study`, `survives_by_project_5pct`, `survives_holm_study_5pct`, `doc`, `note`.
